Further clarification is needed regarding weight/shape concerns (to assess for anorexia) and the presence of binge episodes (to assess for BED).", "is_final": false}

I don’t think it’s that I physically can’t stop, like my body is frozen or something. It’s more… I’m telling myself to stop, and I know I should, but the urge is so strong that my hand just keeps going back before I really get ahead of it.

I guess I worry saying “I could stop if I tried harder” makes it sound like I’m just not trying, and I don’t think that’s fair exactly. But it does feel like I’m watching myself do it and not quite managing to take control in that moment.

But in the moment it really does feel like I’m not fully in charge of stopping, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your hand goes back to the food even though you’re telling yourself to stop, does it feel like you physically can’t stop, or more like you’re struggling with a strong urge but could stop if you tried harder?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I physically can’t stop, like my body is frozen or something. It’s more… I’m telling myself to stop, and I know I should, but the urge is so strong that my hand just keeps going back before I really get ahead of it.\n\nI guess I worry saying “I could stop if I tried harder” makes it sound like I’m just not trying, and I don’t think that’s fair exactly. But it does feel like I’m watching myself do it and not quite managing to take control in that moment.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're eating at night and your hand keeps going back to the food even though you're telling yourself to stop, would you say you feel like you've lost control over your eating in that moment?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ever put on a plate for a normal supper.\n- key: S050\n  name: Loss_of_Control\n  description: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that once I start eating at night, it feels like I can see myself doing it and telling myself to stop, but my hands just keep going back for more.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating and inappropriate compensatory behaviors both occur, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: For the last several months, most weeks have had at least one night where I eat like that and then spend the next day trying to make up for it by skipping meals, setting strict food rules, or taking an extra-long walk.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still managing my job and basic responsibilities, but I’ve been turning down theater and walking-club plans because I feel ashamed and preoccupied with whether I look disciplined enough.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 4 months, since my friend died and my evenings stopped feeling organized.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / architectural_or_engineering_manager / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S050\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so distressed or upset that it affected your ability to function the next day or caused problems in your relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s stopped me from functioning, exactly. I’m still getting to work, still handling the basic responsibilities and all that.\n\nBut… I guess it has affected things a little. I’ve turned down some plans, like going to the theater or meeting up with my walking club. I tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but if I’m honest, part of it is feeling embarrassed and kind of preoccupied with whether I seem… disciplined enough, I guess. That sounds a bit silly saying it out loud, but that’s probably the closest way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating usually happen after you've already gone to sleep and then wake up, or is it more that you're eating a lot later than you intend while still awake?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m still awake… like I haven’t gone to bed properly yet, and it gets later than I meant it to. I don’t usually think of it as waking up from sleep to eat.\n\nI mean, I suppose there could be times I’m tired enough that it all blurs together a bit, but mostly I’m aware I’m up and I just… keep eating later than I intended. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how the nighttime eating makes you feel afterward—like whether it causes you a lot of emotional pain or makes it harder for you to live your life the way you want to?\n\nPatient: Afterward I usually feel pretty ashamed, honestly. I try not to make too much of it because I am still getting up and going to work, and I’m keeping up with the basic things I need to do.\n\nBut it does get in the way more than I’d like to admit. I’ve skipped things I normally enjoy, like theater plans or going with my walking club, because I feel embarrassed and kind of stuck thinking about whether I look like I have enough self-control. I know that may sound odd, but it makes me want to pull back a bit.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel a strong need or compulsion to eat at night, like you have to do it in order to feel okay or relieve a feeling of tension?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.7, F50.0, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is my eating at night. It’s a little embarrassing to say. Once I start, it’s like I’m watching myself do it and even telling myself to stop, but my hands just keep going back for more. I know that probably sounds strange, but… it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night, are you fully awake and aware of what you're doing, or does it feel more like you're on autopilot or not completely in control?\n\nPatient: I’m awake, yes. I know what I’m doing… it’s not like I’m sleepwalking or anything. But it does feel kind of automatic once it starts. Like I can see myself there, and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now,” but then my hands just keep reaching for more anyway. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like your relationships, work, or how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still getting to work, still taking care of the things I need to. So it’s not like everything’s fallen apart or anything. But… yeah, it does bother me more than I probably let on.\n\nI’ve been saying no to some plans—like going to the theater or walking club—because I feel embarrassed, I guess. I get caught up thinking about whether I look like I have enough discipline, if that makes sense. I know that may sound a little silly, but it’s been keeping me from doing things I usually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what it feels like when you say your hands keep reaching for food even when you tell yourself to stop? Does it feel like you can’t control your actions, or more like you just don’t want to stop?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I want to keep going, exactly. I mean, part of me does, I guess, but mostly it feels like… once I’ve started, I can see myself doing it and I’m thinking, “That’s enough, stop,” and then my hand just goes back again anyway.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to describe it that way, because I know I’m the one doing it. It’s not like I’m not aware. But in the moment it really does feel like I’m not fully in charge of stopping, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your hand goes back to the food even though you’re telling yourself to stop, does it feel like you physically can’t stop, or more like you’re struggling with a strong urge but could stop if you tried harder?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I physically can’t stop, like my body is frozen or something. It’s more… I’m telling myself to stop, and I know I should, but the urge is so strong that my hand just keeps going back before I really get ahead of it.\n\nI guess I worry saying “I could stop if I tried harder” makes it sound like I’m just not trying, and I don’t think that’s fair exactly. But it does feel like I’m watching myself do it and not quite managing to take control in that moment.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night and your hand keeps going back to the food even though you're telling yourself to stop, would you say you feel like you've lost control over your eating in that moment?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the right way to say it. I don’t love admitting that, but in the moment it does feel like I’ve lost control over it.\n\nIt’s like I can see myself doing it, and I’m telling myself, “Stop now, that’s enough,” but then my hands just keep going back for more anyway. I know that sounds bad, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset or ashamed that you avoided talking about it, even with people close to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid talking about it with people close to me… I mean, I don’t really bring it up, but I guess I also don’t tend to talk about that kind of thing much anyway.\n\nIt is embarrassing, yes. I don’t like the idea of someone picturing me doing that at night. But I don’t know that I’ve made a conscious decision to hide it so much as… I’d rather not mention it unless I have to. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night and your hands keep going back to the food even though you're telling yourself to stop, does it feel like you're in a different mental state or disconnected from yourself, like you're watching yourself from the outside?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m outside my body watching it happen. I’m aware of what I’m doing, and I know I’m the one doing it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m caught up in it and having a hard time putting the brakes on, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m disconnected or in some other mental state. It’s just… I feel overwhelmed by the urge, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night, do you find yourself doing anything else specific—like needing to eat a certain amount, eating in a particular way, or maybe eating in secret—that feels important or necessary in that moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific rule to it, no. Not like I have to eat a certain number of things or in a certain order.\n\nIt’s more just… I start, and then it’s hard to stop. I might be more private about it because it feels embarrassing, but I don’t know that I’d call it “needing” to eat in secret or anything like that. I haven’t really noticed a particular pattern beyond that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so distressed or upset that it affected your ability to function the next day or caused problems in your relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s stopped me from functioning, exactly. I’m still getting to work, still handling the basic responsibilities and all that.\n\nBut… I guess it has affected things a little. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess it’s… maybe a bit of both, but the part that worries me is once I start. Beforehand I can usually tell myself I’m just going to have something small, or that it’s not a big deal.

But then when I’m actually eating, it feels like I can see myself doing it and I’m thinking, “Alright, stop now,” and I just… don’t. My hands keep going back for more even though I’m telling myself not to. I know that probably sounds like I should be able to just control it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is my eating at night. It’s a little embarrassing to say. Once I start, it’s like I’m watching myself do it and even telling myself to stop, but my hands just keep going back for more. I know that probably sounds strange, but… it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night, are you fully awake and aware of what you're doing, or does it feel more like you're on autopilot or not completely in control?\n\nPatient: I’m awake, yes. I know what I’m doing… it’s not like I’m sleepwalking or anything. But it does feel kind of automatic once it starts. Like I can see myself there, and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now,” but then my hands just keep reaching for more anyway. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like your relationships, work, or how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still getting to work, still taking care of the things I need to. So it’s not like everything’s fallen apart or anything. But… yeah, it does bother me more than I probably let on.\n\nI’ve been saying no to some plans—like going to the theater or walking club—because I feel embarrassed, I guess. I get caught up thinking about whether I look like I have enough discipline, if that makes sense. I know that may sound a little silly, but it’s been keeping me from doing things I usually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what it feels like when you say your hands keep reaching for food even when you tell yourself to stop? Does it feel like you can’t control your actions, or more like you just don’t want to stop?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I want to keep going, exactly. I mean, part of me does, I guess, but mostly it feels like… once I’ve started, I can see myself doing it and I’m thinking, “That’s enough, stop,” and then my hand just goes back again anyway.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to describe it that way, because I know I’m the one doing it. It’s not like I’m not aware. But in the moment it really does feel like I’m not fully in charge of stopping, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your hand goes back to the food even though you’re telling yourself to stop, does it feel like you physically can’t stop, or more like you’re struggling with a strong urge but could stop if you tried harder?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I physically can’t stop, like my body is frozen or something. It’s more… I’m telling myself to stop, and I know I should, but the urge is so strong that my hand just keeps going back before I really get ahead of it.\n\nI guess I worry saying “I could stop if I tried harder” makes it sound like I’m just not trying, and I don’t think that’s fair exactly. But it does feel like I’m watching myself do it and not quite managing to take control in that moment.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night and your hand keeps going back to the food even though you're telling yourself to stop, would you say you feel like you've lost control over your eating in that moment?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the right way to say it. I don’t love admitting that, but in the moment it does feel like I’ve lost control over it.\n\nIt’s like I can see myself doing it, and I’m telling myself, “Stop now, that’s enough,” but then my hands just keep going back for more anyway. I know that sounds bad, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset or ashamed that you avoided talking about it, even with people close to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid talking about it with people close to me… I mean, I don’t really bring it up, but I guess I also don’t tend to talk about that kind of thing much anyway.\n\nIt is embarrassing, yes. I don’t like the idea of someone picturing me doing that at night. But I don’t know that I’ve made a conscious decision to hide it so much as… I’d rather not mention it unless I have to. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night and your hands keep going back to the food even though you're telling yourself to stop, does it feel like you're in a different mental state or disconnected from yourself, like you're watching yourself from the outside?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m outside my body watching it happen. I’m aware of what I’m doing, and I know I’m the one doing it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m caught up in it and having a hard time putting the brakes on, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m disconnected or in some other mental state. It’s just… I feel overwhelmed by the urge, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night, do you find yourself doing anything else specific—like needing to eat a certain amount, eating in a particular way, or maybe eating in secret—that feels important or necessary in that moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific rule to it, no. Not like I have to eat a certain number of things or in a certain order.\n\nIt’s more just… I start, and then it’s hard to stop. I might be more private about it because it feels embarrassing, but I don’t know that I’d call it “needing” to eat in secret or anything like that. I haven’t really noticed a particular pattern beyond that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so distressed or upset that it affected your ability to function the next day or caused problems in your relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s stopped me from functioning, exactly. I’m still getting to work, still handling the basic responsibilities and all that.\n\nBut… I guess it has affected things a little. I’ve turned down some plans, like going to the theater or meeting up with my walking club. I tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but if I’m honest, part of it is feeling embarrassed and kind of preoccupied with whether I seem… disciplined enough, I guess. That sounds a bit silly saying it out loud, but that’s probably the closest way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating usually happen after you've already gone to sleep and then wake up, or is it more that you're eating a lot later than you intend while still awake?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m still awake… like I haven’t gone to bed properly yet, and it gets later than I meant it to. It’s more like I feel this pull, and stopping feels harder than it should. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens right before you start eating at night? Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is my eating at night. It’s a little embarrassing to say. Once I start, it’s like I’m watching myself do it and even telling myself to stop, but my hands just keep going back for more. I know that probably sounds strange, but… it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night, are you fully awake and aware of what you're doing, or does it feel more like you're on autopilot or not completely in control?\n\nPatient: I’m awake, yes. I know what I’m doing… it’s not like I’m sleepwalking or anything. But it does feel kind of automatic once it starts. Like I can see myself there, and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now,” but then my hands just keep reaching for more anyway. I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your daily life in any way, like your relationships, work, or how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still getting to work, still taking care of the things I need to. So it’s not like everything’s fallen apart or anything. But… yeah, it does bother me more than I probably let on.\n\nI’ve been saying no to some plans—like going to the theater or walking club—because I feel embarrassed, I guess. I get caught up thinking about whether I look like I have enough discipline, if that makes sense. I know that may sound a little silly, but it’s been keeping me from doing things I usually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what it feels like when you say your hands keep reaching for food even when you tell yourself to stop? Does it feel like you can’t control your actions, or more like you just don’t want to stop?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I want to keep going, exactly. I mean, part of me does, I guess, but mostly it feels like… once I’ve started, I can see myself doing it and I’m thinking, “That’s enough, stop,” and then my hand just goes back again anyway.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to describe it that way, because I know I’m the one doing it. It’s not like I’m not aware. But in the moment it really does feel like I’m not fully in charge of stopping, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When your hand goes back to the food even though you’re telling yourself to stop, does it feel like you physically can’t stop, or more like you’re struggling with a strong urge but could stop if you tried harder?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I physically can’t stop, like my body is frozen or something. It’s more… I’m telling myself to stop, and I know I should, but the urge is so strong that my hand just keeps going back before I really get ahead of it.\n\nI guess I worry saying “I could stop if I tried harder” makes it sound like I’m just not trying, and I don’t think that’s fair exactly. But it does feel like I’m watching myself do it and not quite managing to take control in that moment.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night and your hand keeps going back to the food even though you're telling yourself to stop, would you say you feel like you've lost control over your eating in that moment?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the right way to say it. I don’t love admitting that, but in the moment it does feel like I’ve lost control over it.\n\nIt’s like I can see myself doing it, and I’m telling myself, “Stop now, that’s enough,” but then my hands just keep going back for more anyway. I know that sounds bad, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so upset or ashamed that you avoided talking about it, even with people close to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid talking about it with people close to me… I mean, I don’t really bring it up, but I guess I also don’t tend to talk about that kind of thing much anyway.\n\nIt is embarrassing, yes. I don’t like the idea of someone picturing me doing that at night. But I don’t know that I’ve made a conscious decision to hide it so much as… I’d rather not mention it unless I have to. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night and your hands keep going back to the food even though you're telling yourself to stop, does it feel like you're in a different mental state or disconnected from yourself, like you're watching yourself from the outside?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m outside my body watching it happen. I’m aware of what I’m doing, and I know I’m the one doing it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m caught up in it and having a hard time putting the brakes on, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m disconnected or in some other mental state. It’s just… I feel overwhelmed by the urge, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night, do you find yourself doing anything else specific—like needing to eat a certain amount, eating in a particular way, or maybe eating in secret—that feels important or necessary in that moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific rule to it, no. Not like I have to eat a certain number of things or in a certain order.\n\nIt’s more just… I start, and then it’s hard to stop. I might be more private about it because it feels embarrassing, but I don’t know that I’d call it “needing” to eat in secret or anything like that. I haven’t really noticed a particular pattern beyond that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so distressed or upset that it affected your ability to function the next day or caused problems in your relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s stopped me from functioning, exactly. I’m still getting to work, still handling the basic responsibilities and all that.\n\nBut… I guess it has affected things a little. I’ve turned down some plans, like going to the theater or meeting up with my walking club. I tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but if I’m honest, part of it is feeling embarrassed and kind of preoccupied with whether I seem… disciplined enough, I guess. That sounds a bit silly saying it out loud, but that’s probably the closest way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating usually happen after you've already gone to sleep and then wake up, or is it more that you're eating a lot later than you intend while still awake?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m still awake… like I haven’t gone to bed properly yet, and it gets later than I meant it to. I don’t usually think of it as waking up from sleep to eat.\n\nI mean, I suppose there could be times I’m tired enough that it all blurs together a bit, but mostly I’m aware I’m up and I just… keep eating later than I intended. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how the nighttime eating makes you feel afterward—like whether it causes you a lot of emotional pain or makes it harder for you to live your life the way you want to?\n\nPatient: Afterward I usually feel pretty ashamed, honestly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night and your hands keep going back to the food even though you're telling yourself to stop, does it feel like you're in a different mental state or disconnected from yourself, like you're watching yourself from the outside?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like I’m outside my body watching it happen. I’m aware of what I’m doing, and I know I’m the one doing it.\n\nIt’s more like I’m caught up in it and having a hard time putting the brakes on, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’m disconnected or in some other mental state. It’s just… I feel overwhelmed by the urge, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night, do you find yourself doing anything else specific—like needing to eat a certain amount, eating in a particular way, or maybe eating in secret—that feels important or necessary in that moment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific rule to it, no. Not like I have to eat a certain number of things or in a certain order.\n\nIt’s more just… I start, and then it’s hard to stop. I might be more private about it because it feels embarrassing, but I don’t know that I’d call it “needing” to eat in secret or anything like that. I haven’t really noticed a particular pattern beyond that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Has this nighttime eating ever made you feel so distressed or upset that it affected your ability to function the next day or caused problems in your relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s stopped me from functioning, exactly. I’m still getting to work, still handling the basic responsibilities and all that.\n\nBut… I guess it has affected things a little. I’ve turned down some plans, like going to the theater or meeting up with my walking club. I tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but if I’m honest, part of it is feeling embarrassed and kind of preoccupied with whether I seem… disciplined enough, I guess. That sounds a bit silly saying it out loud, but that’s probably the closest way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Does this nighttime eating usually happen after you've already gone to sleep and then wake up, or is it more that you're eating a lot later than you intend while still awake?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m still awake… like I haven’t gone to bed properly yet, and it gets later than I meant it to. I don’t usually think of it as waking up from sleep to eat.\n\nI mean, I suppose there could be times I’m tired enough that it all blurs together a bit, but mostly I’m aware I’m up and I just… keep eating later than I intended. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how the nighttime eating makes you feel afterward—like whether it causes you a lot of emotional pain or makes it harder for you to live your life the way you want to?\n\nPatient: Afterward I usually feel pretty ashamed, honestly. I try not to make too much of it because I am still getting up and going to work, and I’m keeping up with the basic things I need to do.\n\nBut it does get in the way more than I’d like to admit. I’ve skipped things I normally enjoy, like theater plans or going with my walking club, because I feel embarrassed and kind of stuck thinking about whether I look like I have enough self-control. I know that may sound odd, but it makes me want to pull back a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a strong need or compulsion to eat at night, like you have to do it in order to feel okay or relieve a feeling of tension?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t love admitting it, but once I start eating at night, it can feel like I’m watching myself do it and telling myself, “Okay, stop now,” but then my hands just keep going back for more.\n\nIt’s not exactly that I want to keep doing it. It’s more like I feel this pull, and stopping feels harder than it should. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens right before you start eating at night? Do you feel a strong, almost irresistible need to eat, or does it happen more impulsively without that sense of needing to do it?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… maybe a bit of both, but the part that worries me is once I start. Beforehand I can usually tell myself I’m just going to have something small, or that it’s not a big deal.\n\nBut then when I’m actually eating, it feels like I can see myself doing it and I’m thinking, “Alright, stop now,” and I just… don’t. My hands keep going back for more even though I’m telling myself not to. I know that probably sounds like I should be able to just control it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple.\n\nDoctor: When you start eating at night, would you say you feel a complete loss of control over your eating, like you can’t stop once you’ve started, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would say that, though “complete” feels a little hard to admit. It’s not like I’m unaware of what I’m doing. I can see myself eating, and I’m telling myself, “Stop now, that’s enough.”\n\nBut then my hands just keep going back for more anyway. So… yes, in that moment it feels like I can’t really stop, even though I want to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
